I crave a good night's rest. It's not that I feel like I only need two hours of sleep and I'm ready to take on the world—no, no, no. I feel the physical exhaustion right down in my bones. I'm not euphoric or feeling like Superman or anything like that. It's much more of a frustrating feeling, you know? It’s like being trapped on a treadmill that someone else is controlling, and they've cranked the speed all the way up, and you're just trying not to fall flat on your face. So, no, definitely not unusually happy or feeling like I don't need to rest. I desperately want to rest, I just... I mean, I'm frustrated, absolutely. Like I was saying, it's exhausting being this wound up all the time. But it's not a sad feeling, you know? It's not like I'm waking up in the morning and thinking, \"Oh, what's the point of it all?\" or feeling like a dark cloud is hanging over me. I actually really care about my job and my students. Just last week, one of my kids—this little guy who's been struggling so much with his reading comprehension—he finally had this breakthrough moment with a story we were working on, and I felt so genuinely happy and proud of him. I still get a lot of joy out of things like that. \n\nIt's just that the joy is sort of... overshadowed, I guess, by this constant need to be doing something else at the same time. Like, I was thrilled for him, but while I was telling him \"good job,\" I was also simultaneously rearranging the dry erase markers on the whiteboard ledge and thinking about how I needed to go fix that squeaky wheel on the AV cart down the hall. So, no, I don't feel down or hopeless in that heavy, depressed kind of way. It's really just the physical and mental toll of not being able to stop moving that's getting to me. I still look forward to things, I still enjoy my wife's cooking, I still like sitting on the porch—well, trying to sit on the porch—it's just the motor won't quit. But no, not depressed. Just tired of the motor.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts or feeling driven to perform certain behaviors over and over again, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that. I know what you mean, like... like having to wash your hands a dozen times or checking the stove over and over before you can leave the house. My brother-in-law, actually, he used to do that thing where he'd tap the doorframe three times before he walked through it, every single time. Drove my sister absolutely up the wall, let me tell you. But no, I don't have anything like that. I'm not doing things because I have some... some thought stuck in my head telling me something bad will happen if I don't. \n\nIt's really not about the thoughts at all. It's just the physical need to be doing *something*. Like I said about the supply closet—I'm not organizing it because I'm obsessed with the markers being in color order or because I can't stop thinking about the glue sticks. I organize it because I happen to be standing near it, and my hands just need to be moving, and I can't just stand there doing nothing. If the closet wasn't there, I'd be fixing a chair, or pacing, or, I don't know, wiping down the chalkboard. It doesn't matter what the task is, really, as long as it's a task. \n\nAnd my thoughts... well, they aren't stuck on one thing, that's for sure. If anything, they're bouncing all over the place just like I am. I'll be thinking about a student's reading level, and then suddenly I'm thinking about whether I need to change the oil in the lawnmower, and then I'm wondering if we have enough coffee filters at home. It's just a constant stream of different things, never the same thought over and over like a broken record. So, no, I wouldn't say I have those intrusive thoughts or feel forced to do the same exact behavior repeatedly. I just... I just have to keep moving, whatever that movement happens to be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was unusually elevated, irritable, or you felt more energetic than usual for several days in a row, to the point where others noticed you were different?\n\nPatient: Elevated or more energetic than usual for a few days? No, no. Because, well, what's \"usual\" for me, right? My \"usual\" is already running at a hundred miles an hour. It's not like I have these... these spells, or phases, where suddenly for three or four days I'm bouncing off the walls and then I go back to being calm. The bouncing off the walls *is* the normal. Honestly, if I suddenly sat still for three days, *that* would be when people would notice I was different! My wife, we've been married thirty-two years, and she always jokes that she's been waiting for my batteries to run out since 1991. So it's not a sudden spike in energy that comes out of nowhere. It's just... always there. Every single day. \n\nAnd as for irritable... I mean, sure, I get irritable, but it's entirely situational. It's not a mood that just takes over my life for days on end. Like, I get incredibly irritable if I'm stuck in one of those mandatory district professional development seminars. You know the ones? Where they cram fifty of us teachers into a stuffy school library, the chairs are those awful hard plastic things, and the presenter is just reading word-for-word off a PowerPoint slide for three hours straight? Oh, I absolutely can't stand that. My knee starts bouncing, I'm clicking my pen, I'm getting up to \"use the restroom\" or get water four or five times just to walk down the hall... I get very irritable then, because I just feel completely trapped in that chair. \n\nBut the minute that meeting is over and I can get back to my classroom and start setting up the sensory bins for my kids or organizing the reading corner, the irritability is completely gone. It vanishes. So it's not a several-day mood thing, it's just me reacting to having to sit still. And I'm definitely not walking around feeling \"elevated\" or like I won the lottery. Like I was telling you before, I'm just exhausted by it all. The energy is physical, it's just this constant motor running in the background. Monday through Sunday, fifty-two weeks a year. So, no, I haven't had any distinct periods where my mood or energy suddenly shifted like you're talking about. It's just the same old exhausting baseline.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting two weeks or more when you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? No. No, like I was saying a few minutes ago when you asked about feeling depressed, I really don't get like that. I've definitely never had a stretch of two whole weeks where I just felt down or stopped caring about things. \n\nHonestly, it’s the exact opposite when it comes to my interests. I never lose interest in things—if anything, I have *too many* interests and I just can't stick to one for very long before I'm up and doing something else. Take my garage, for example. I love woodworking. Always have. I've been building this birdhouse—well, it started as a birdhouse, then I decided it was going to be a whole elaborate feeding station for the blue jays we get in the backyard. My wife, she loves watching the birds from the kitchen window while she has her morning tea, so I figured, why not make something really nice for her to look at, right? \n\nAnyway, I haven't lost interest in it at all. I still want to finish it. I still love the idea of it. But I'll be out there sanding the roof of this thing, and then I'll notice the weed whacker needs new string, so I start doing that, and then I see a crack in the driveway that needs patching... but the *interest* in the woodworking is still 100 percent there! I never wake up and think, \"I don't care about the birds anymore\" or \"I don't want to go to work.\" I love my students. I told you about that little guy with his reading breakthrough earlier—no, I never lose that spark for the things I care about. \n\nSo a whole two weeks of just feeling down and not caring? No, never. I mean, sure, I might have a bad day here and there, who doesn't? Like when the school board cut our budget for special education sensory tools last year. I was pretty upset for a couple of days, fighting with the administration, writing emails late into the night, pacing around the living room trying to figure out how I was going to afford the supplies out of my own pocket. But I didn't lose interest or get depressed. If anything, I was more fired up. I just don't have that... that shut-down setting, I guess. I'm always on. Even when I'm completely exhausted from being on, I'm still on. So no, no two-week slumps for me. I just keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things like hearing voices that others don’t hear, or seeing things that others don’t see? Or maybe had thoughts that felt very strange or not your own?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, no, nothing like that. The only voices I hear are my students, and that’s just because when you spend all day with a room full of kids who need a lot of extra support, their questions just sort of echo in your ears even after you clock out. You know, like when you leave a loud concert and you can still hear the bass? It’s like that, but with questions about fractions. But actual voices that no one else can hear? No, never. \n\nAnd seeing things, definitely not. Honestly, I have a hard enough time keeping track of the things that actually are there! Half the time I’m looking for my keys or my favorite red grading pen, and they’re right in front of my face. If I started seeing things that weren't even real on top of all that, I don't know how I'd ever get out the door in the morning. \n\nAs for the thoughts... you mean like someone else is putting them in my head? No, they are all mine. Unfortunately, they are all mine. I was actually just saying to my wife the other evening—she was sitting out on the patio with her tea, and I was sweeping the deck because the oak tree out back just drops these little twigs everywhere, it’s a never-ending battle keeping that wood clean—and I told her that I wished I could just hand my brain over to someone else for the weekend. Just let someone else drive for a bit so I could get some peace. Because my thoughts are just so crowded. But they are definitely my thoughts. It’s just that there are a hundred of them at once. I’ll be thinking about whether I need to rotate the tires on the sedan, and then I’m worrying about a student’s individualized education plan for Thursday, and then I’m trying to remember if we have enough dog food. It’s all very normal, boring stuff, just happening way too fast. None of it feels alien or strange or anything like that. So no, I’ve never had anything like what you’re talking about. Just the usual traffic jam up there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, even if you're not necessarily feeling sad or down?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Ha! No, no, I don't think I even have a slow gear. If anything, my wife says I'm like a wind-up toy that someone overwound and broke the knob off. But trouble sitting still? Oh, absolutely. That’s... well, that’s just the story of my life right there. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about those professional development days, it’s just impossible for me to just exist in a chair. Especially in IEP meetings—Individualized Education Program meetings, you know, where we sit down with the parents, the school psychologist, maybe the speech therapist... these meetings are incredibly important for the kids. They really map out their whole academic year and what accommodations they need, so I really want to be completely focused and professional. But my body just will not cooperate. \n\nWe’ll be sitting around that big fake-wood conference table in the main office, and my leg is just bouncing under the table. Just going a mile a minute. Or I’m clicking a pen. Click-click-click. And I’m shifting in the chair constantly—crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning forward, leaning back, adjusting my posture. I do it so much that people actually glance over at me. The parents or the principal will just stop talking for a second and look, and it's only when they give me that look that I even realize I’m doing it! I don't even know I'm moving until someone's eyes lock onto my clicking pen or my shaking knee. \n\nAnd it’s not just the IEP meetings, it happens in my admin classes, too. You know, the continuing education classes we have to take to keep our teaching certificates current? Same exact thing. I'm just vibrating in the seat the whole time, trying to find a comfortable position that doesn't exist. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I'm fifty-eight years old, I should be able to sit through a forty-five-minute meeting without squirming like one of my second graders, but I just... I can't. The energy just has to go somewhere. So, definitely not moving slowly, no, but sitting still is pretty much a monumental task for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt unusually high, overly active, or extremely productive for several days or more, even if it didn’t last very long?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or extremely productive? Gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm always high energy, like I was just saying about the meetings and the bouncing leg and all that. That's just my baseline. It's not some special period that comes and goes for a few days, you know? It's every single day. From the minute I wake up at 5:30 in the morning—and I always wake up at 5:30, usually before the alarm even goes off, much to my wife's dismay because I immediately start puttering around the kitchen making coffee and accidentally dropping spoons or clanking mugs—to the minute I finally crash at night. It's just constant. \n\nAnd as for extremely productive... boy, I wish! If I had a few days where I was actually extremely productive, I might actually finish that birdhouse I was telling you about. Or I might finally get through the backlog of progress reports for my students. You see, I'm overly active, sure, I'll give you that one. But active does not equal productive. Not in my world, anyway. \n\nJust this past weekend, for instance, I had this whole plan. I was going to grade a stack of math worksheets, clean out the gutters, and organize the recycling in the garage. Sounds like a very productive Saturday, right? Well, I took the worksheets out to the patio table, sat down, graded exactly three of them, and then I noticed the gutters were looking really full of those oak leaves I mentioned earlier. So I went to get the ladder. But while I was getting the ladder from the garage, I saw the recycling bins were overflowing, so I started breaking down cardboard boxes. Halfway through breaking down the boxes, the dog started barking at the mail carrier, so I went to check the mail, and then I ended up talking to my neighbor Bob about his new riding lawnmower for twenty minutes because he was having trouble with the spark plugs. \n\nBy the end of the day, I was completely exhausted. I hadn't stopped moving for ten hours straight. But the gutters were still full, half-ripped cardboard was scattered all over the driveway, and those math worksheets were blowing around on the patio table. \n\nSo, no, I definitely don't have periods where I feel unusually high or get a ton of stuff done. I don't have those... those magical phases where everything clicks and I'm a machine. I'm just constantly buzzing around, always in motion, but it's not a special episode or anything. It's just my normal, chaotic everyday life. I just have this constant hum of doing a little bit of everything all at once, but never really finishing anything. So no, nothing like what you're describing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, boy... yes. Yes, they really have. It’s actually gotten to a point where I’m pretty worried about how things are going, to be completely honest with you. 

At work, for instance... you know how I mentioned the IEP meetings and the admin classes earlier, and how I just can't seem to sit still? Well, it’s not just a quirky annoyance anymore. The complaints from the other staff and even some parents have gotten serious enough that my principal actually pulled me into her office last week. She closed the door—which is never a good sign in a school building, let me tell you—and she told me that I was being put on a formal corrective plan. A corrective plan! I’m fifty-eight years old, I’ve been a special education teacher for decades, and now I’ve got paperwork in my file because I can’t stop being a disruption. It was a real blow to my pride, but I see their point. I'm just so all over the place.

And it’s definitely spilling over at home, too. My wife, she is an absolute saint, but she is just exhausted. We help out a lot with her elderly mother—getting her groceries, sorting her pills, helping around her house—and my wife actually sat me down the other night and said that doing those caregiving routines is just getting so much harder for her. She told me, point blank, that it’s because she feels like she has to manage my energy on top of everything else. 

Like, she’ll be trying to focus on getting her mom’s schedule sorted for the week, and I’ll be pacing around the kitchen, talking a mile a minute, starting to fix a leaky faucet but then leaving all the tools on the counter to go check the mail... and it just overwhelms her. She said I'm like a tornado in a space where she desperately needs some calm. 

It’s just... it’s a terrible feeling. I really feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m trying to help! I really am. I want to be a supportive husband, I want to be a good teacher for these kids, but it seems like the harder I try to pitch in, the more chaos I end up causing for the people around me. They just end up having to manage me, instead of me actually being helpful. So, yes, it's definitely causing some very real problems.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, doctor, it's... it's just getting to be a bit much lately. I'm a special education teacher, and you know, you'd think after all these years—I'm 58 now—you'd think I'd be slowing down a bit, maybe taking it a little easier, but it's actually the exact opposite. What really brings me in today is this... this overwhelming feeling like I am just constantly running on a motor. It’s exactly like that, just a literal engine humming inside my chest that just won't cut out. \n\nI mean, I’ll be at school, right? And I'll finish up an IEP meeting or get done setting up a lesson for my kids—which takes a lot of energy, let me tell you, keeping up with them—but the second I'm done, boom, I immediately jump into the next thing. I don't even sit down to take a breath. I'm rearranging the desks, I'm pacing the room, I'm organizing the supply closet that I just organized on Tuesday. And you'd think I'd go home and just collapse on the couch, right? But no, I get home and it's the exact same story. I walk through the door, drop my bag, and I'm instantly fixing a hinge on the kitchen cabinet, or I'm out doing yard work in the dark, or... I just leap from one task to the next without ever, ever being able to shut myself off. \n\nIt’s like the off switch is completely broken. I just keep going and going and going. My wife says it's exhausting just watching me, and frankly, it's exhausting being me right now, because I just can't seem to stop moving or find any sort of pause button, no matter how much I might want to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually upbeat, overly confident, or like you don't need as much sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, definitely not upbeat. Honestly, if anything, it's the exact opposite—I'm just so tired of it. You know, people see me moving around all the time at the school, pacing the halls or constantly rearranging the classroom, and they probably think, \"Oh, look at him, he's got so much energy, he's so enthusiastic about his job!\" But it's not enthusiasm. It's not like I'm walking around feeling like I'm on top of the world or that I can conquer anything. I don't feel overly confident at all, really. Most days I feel like I'm barely keeping my head above water because of this constant, nagging drive to just keep doing things. \n\nAnd the sleep thing... boy, I wish I felt like I didn't need sleep. I definitely, definitely need it. I mean, I'm 58 years old, my joints feel it, my lower back absolutely feels it from leaning over those lower desks and chasing after my students all day. I crave a good night's rest. It's not that I feel like I only need two hours of sleep and I'm ready to take on the world—no, no, no. I feel the physical exhaustion right down in my bones. I'm not euphoric or feeling like Superman or anything like that. It's much more of a frustrating feeling, you know? It’s like being trapped on a treadmill that someone else is controlling, and they've cranked the speed all the way up, and you're just trying not to fall flat on your face. So, no, definitely not unusually happy or feeling like I don't need to rest. I desperately want to rest, I just... I just can't seem to make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. I mean, I'm frustrated, absolutely. Like I was saying, it's exhausting being this wound up all the time. But it's not a sad feeling, you know? It's not like I'm waking up in the morning and thinking, \"Oh, what's the point of it all?\" or feeling like a dark cloud is hanging over me. I actually really care about my job and my students. Just last week, one of my kids—this little guy who's been struggling so much with his reading comprehension—he finally had this breakthrough moment with a story we were working on, and I felt so genuinely happy and proud of him. I still get a lot of joy out of things like that. \n\nIt's just that the joy is sort of... overshadowed, I guess, by this constant need to be doing something else at the same time. Like, I was thrilled for him, but while I was telling him \"good job,\" I was also simultaneously rearranging the dry erase markers on the whiteboard ledge and thinking about how I needed to go fix that squeaky wheel on the AV cart down the hall. So, no, I don't feel down or hopeless in that heavy, depressed kind of way. It's really just the physical and mental toll of not being able to stop moving that's getting to me. I still look forward to things, I still enjoy my wife's cooking, I still like sitting on the porch—well, trying to sit on the porch—it's just the motor won't quit. But no, not depressed. Just tired of the motor.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts or feeling driven to perform certain behaviors over and over again, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that. I know what you mean, like... like having to wash your hands a dozen times or checking the stove over and over before you can leave the house. My brother-in-law, actually, he used to do that thing where he'd tap the doorframe three times before he walked through it, every single time. Drove my sister absolutely up the wall, let me tell you. But no, I don't have anything like that. I'm not doing things because I have some... some thought stuck in my head telling me something bad will happen if I don't. \n\nIt's really not about the thoughts at all. It's just the physical need to be doing *something*. Like I said about the supply closet—I'm not organizing it because I'm obsessed with the markers being in color order or because I can't stop thinking about the glue sticks. I organize it because I happen to be standing near it, and my hands just need to be moving, and I can't just stand there doing nothing. If the closet wasn't there, I'd be fixing a chair, or pacing, or, I don't know, wiping down the chalkboard. It doesn't matter what the task is, really, as long as it's a task. \n\nAnd my thoughts... well, they aren't stuck on one thing, that's for sure. If anything, they're bouncing all over the place just like I am. I'll be thinking about a student's reading level, and then suddenly I'm thinking about whether I need to change the oil in the lawnmower, and then I'm wondering if we have enough coffee filters at home. It's just a constant stream of different things, never the same thought over and over like a broken record. So, no, I wouldn't say I have those intrusive thoughts or feel forced to do the same exact behavior repeatedly. I just... I just have to keep moving, whatever that movement happens to be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was unusually elevated, irritable, or you felt more energetic than usual for several days in a row, to the point where others noticed you were different?\n\nPatient: Elevated or more energetic than usual for a few days? No, no. Because, well, what's \"usual\" for me, right? My \"usual\" is already running at a hundred miles an hour. It's not like I have these... these spells, or phases, where suddenly for three or four days I'm bouncing off the walls and then I go back to being calm. The bouncing off the walls *is* the normal. Honestly, if I suddenly sat still for three days, *that* would be when people would notice I was different! My wife, we've been married thirty-two years, and she always jokes that she's been waiting for my batteries to run out since 1991. So it's not a sudden spike in energy that comes out of nowhere. It's just... always there. Every single day. \n\nAnd as for irritable... I mean, sure, I get irritable, but it's entirely situational. It's not a mood that just takes over my life for days on end. Like, I get incredibly irritable if I'm stuck in one of those mandatory district professional development seminars. You know the ones? Where they cram fifty of us teachers into a stuffy school library, the chairs are those awful hard plastic things, and the presenter is just reading word-for-word off a PowerPoint slide for three hours straight? Oh, I absolutely can't stand that. My knee starts bouncing, I'm clicking my pen, I'm getting up to \"use the restroom\" or get water four or five times just to walk down the hall... I get very irritable then, because I just feel completely trapped in that chair. \n\nBut the minute that meeting is over and I can get back to my classroom and start setting up the sensory bins for my kids or organizing the reading corner, the irritability is completely gone. It vanishes. So it's not a several-day mood thing, it's just me reacting to having to sit still. And I'm definitely not walking around feeling \"elevated\" or like I won the lottery. Like I was telling you before, I'm just exhausted by it all. The energy is physical, it's just this constant motor running in the background. Monday through Sunday, fifty-two weeks a year. So, no, I haven't had any distinct periods where my mood or energy suddenly shifted like you're talking about. It's just the same old exhausting baseline.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting two weeks or more when you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? No. No, like I was saying a few minutes ago when you asked about feeling depressed, I really don't get like that. I've definitely never had a stretch of two whole weeks where I just felt down or stopped caring about things. \n\nHonestly, it’s the exact opposite when it comes to my interests. I never lose interest in things—if anything, I have *too many* interests and I just can't stick to one for very long before I'm up and doing something else. Take my garage, for example. I love woodworking. Always have. I've been building this birdhouse—well, it started as a birdhouse, then I decided it was going to be a whole elaborate feeding station for the blue jays we get in the backyard. My wife, she loves watching the birds from the kitchen window while she has her morning tea, so I figured, why not make something really nice for her to look at, right? \n\nAnyway, I haven't lost interest in it at all. I still want to finish it. I still love the idea of it. But I'll be out there sanding the roof of this thing, and then I'll notice the weed whacker needs new string, so I start doing that, and then I see a crack in the driveway that needs patching... but the *interest* in the woodworking is still 100 percent there! I never wake up and think, \"I don't care about the birds anymore\" or \"I don't want to go to work.\" I love my students. I told you about that little guy with his reading breakthrough earlier—no, I never lose that spark for the things I care about. \n\nSo a whole two weeks of just feeling down and not caring? No, never. I mean, sure, I might have a bad day here and there, who doesn't? Like when the school board cut our budget for special education sensory tools last year. I was pretty upset for a couple of days, fighting with the administration, writing emails late into the night, pacing around the living room trying to figure out how I was going to afford the supplies out of my own pocket. But I didn't lose interest or get depressed. If anything, I was more fired up. I just don't have that... that shut-down setting, I guess. I'm always on. Even when I'm completely exhausted from being on, I'm still on. So no, no two-week slumps for me. I just keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things like hearing voices that others don’t hear, or seeing things that others don’t see? Or maybe had thoughts that felt very strange or not your own?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, no, nothing like that. The only voices I hear are my students, and that’s just because when you spend all day with a room full of kids who need a lot of extra support, their questions just sort of echo in your ears even after you clock out. You know, like when you leave a loud concert and you can still hear the bass? It’s like that, but with questions about fractions. But actual voices that no one else can hear? No, never. \n\nAnd seeing things, definitely not. Honestly, I have a hard enough time keeping track of the things that actually are there! Half the time I’m looking for my keys or my favorite red grading pen, and they’re right in front of my face. If I started seeing things that weren't even real on top of all that, I don't know how I'd ever get out the door in the morning. \n\nAs for the thoughts... you mean like someone else is putting them in my head? No, they are all mine. Unfortunately, they are all mine. I was actually just saying to my wife the other evening—she was sitting out on the patio with her tea, and I was sweeping the deck because the oak tree out back just drops these little twigs everywhere, it’s a never-ending battle keeping that wood clean—and I told her that I wished I could just hand my brain over to someone else for the weekend. Just let someone else drive for a bit so I could get some peace. Because my thoughts are just so crowded. But they are definitely my thoughts. It’s just that there are a hundred of them at once. I’ll be thinking about whether I need to rotate the tires on the sedan, and then I’m worrying about a student’s individualized education plan for Thursday, and then I’m trying to remember if we have enough dog food. It’s all very normal, boring stuff, just happening way too fast. None of it feels alien or strange or anything like that. So no, I’ve never had anything like what you’re talking about. Just the usual traffic jam up there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, even if you're not necessarily feeling sad or down?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Ha! No, no, I don't think I even have a slow gear. If anything, my wife says I'm like a wind-up toy that someone overwound and broke the knob off. But trouble sitting still? Oh, absolutely. That’s... well, that’s just the story of my life right there. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about those professional development days, it’s just impossible for me to just exist in a chair. Especially in IEP meetings—Individualized Education Program meetings, you know, where we sit down with the parents, the school psychologist, maybe the speech therapist... these meetings are incredibly important for the kids. They really map out their whole academic year and what accommodations they need, so I really want to be completely focused and professional. But my body just will not cooperate. \n\nWe’ll be sitting around that big fake-wood conference table in the main office, and my leg is just bouncing under the table. Just going a mile a minute. Or I’m clicking a pen. Click-click-click. And I’m shifting in the chair constantly—crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning forward, leaning back, adjusting my posture. I do it so much that people actually glance over at me. The parents or the principal will just stop talking for a second and look, and it's only when they give me that look that I even realize I’m doing it! I don't even know I'm moving until someone's eyes lock onto my clicking pen or my shaking knee. \n\nAnd it’s not just the IEP meetings, it happens in my admin classes, too. You know, the continuing education classes we have to take to keep our teaching certificates current? Same exact thing. I'm just vibrating in the seat the whole time, trying to find a comfortable position that doesn't exist. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I'm fifty-eight years old, I should be able to sit through a forty-five-minute meeting without squirming like one of my second graders, but I just... I can't. The energy just has to go somewhere. So, definitely not moving slowly, no, but sitting still is pretty much a monumental task for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt unusually high, overly active, or extremely productive for several days or more, even if it didn’t last very long?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or extremely productive? Gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm always high energy, like I was just saying about the meetings and the bouncing leg and all that. That's just my baseline. It's not some special period that comes and goes for a few days, you know? It's every single day. From the minute I wake up at 5:30 in the morning—and I always wake up at 5:30, usually before the alarm even goes off, much to my wife's dismay because I immediately start puttering around the kitchen making coffee and accidentally dropping spoons or clanking mugs—to the minute I finally crash at night. It's just constant. \n\nAnd as for extremely productive... boy, I wish! If I had a few days where I was actually extremely productive, I might actually finish that birdhouse I was telling you about. Or I might finally get through the backlog of progress reports for my students. You see, I'm overly active, sure, I'll give you that one. But active does not equal productive. Not in my world, anyway. \n\nJust this past weekend, for instance, I had this whole plan. I was going to grade a stack of math worksheets, clean out the gutters, and organize the recycling in the garage. Sounds like a very productive Saturday, right? Well, I took the worksheets out to the patio table, sat down, graded exactly three of them, and then I noticed the gutters were looking really full of those oak leaves I mentioned earlier. So I went to get the ladder. But while I was getting the ladder from the garage, I saw the recycling bins were overflowing, so I started breaking down cardboard boxes. Halfway through breaking down the boxes, the dog started barking at the mail carrier, so I went to check the mail, and then I ended up talking to my neighbor Bob about his new riding lawnmower for twenty minutes because he was having trouble with the spark plugs. \n\nBy the end of the day, I was completely exhausted. I hadn't stopped moving for ten hours straight. But the gutters were still full, half-ripped cardboard was scattered all over the driveway, and those math worksheets were blowing around on the patio table. \n\nSo, no, I definitely don't have periods where I feel unusually high or get a ton of stuff done. I don't have those... those magical phases where everything clicks and I'm a machine. I'm just constantly buzzing around, always in motion, but it's not a special episode or anything. It's just my normal, chaotic everyday life. I just have this constant hum of doing a little bit of everything all at once, but never really finishing anything. So no, nothing like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant problems in your daily life, such as at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, boy... yes. Yes, they really have. It’s actually gotten to a point where I’m pretty worried about how things are going, to be completely honest with you. \n\nAt work, for instance... you know how I mentioned the IEP meetings and the admin classes earlier, and how I just can't seem to sit still? Well, it’s not just a quirky annoyance anymore. The complaints from the other staff and even some parents have gotten serious enough that my principal actually pulled me into her office last week. She closed the door—which is never a good sign in a school building, let me tell you—and she told me that I was being put on a formal corrective plan. A corrective plan! I’m fifty-eight years old, I’ve been a special education teacher for decades, and now I’ve got paperwork in my file because I can’t stop being a disruption. It was a real blow to my pride, but I see their point. I'm just so all over the place.\n\nAnd it’s definitely spilling over at home, too. My wife, she is an absolute saint, but she is just exhausted. We help out a lot with her elderly mother—getting her groceries, sorting her pills, helping around her house—and my wife actually sat me down the other night and said that doing those caregiving routines is just getting so much harder for her. She told me, point blank, that it’s because she feels like she has to manage my energy on top of everything else. \n\nLike, she’ll be trying to focus on getting her mom’s schedule sorted for the week, and I’ll be pacing around the kitchen, talking a mile a minute, starting to fix a leaky faucet but then leaving all the tools on the counter to go check the mail... and it just overwhelms her. She said I'm like a tornado in a space where she desperately needs some calm. \n\nIt’s just... it’s a terrible feeling. I really feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m trying to help! I really am. I want to be a supportive husband, I want to be a good teacher for these kids, but it seems like the harder I try to pitch in, the more chaos I end up causing for the people around me. They just end up having to manage me, instead of me actually being helpful. So, yes, it's definitely causing some very real problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the minute I wake up at 5:30 in the morning—and I always wake up at 5:30, usually before the alarm even goes off, much to my wife's dismay because I immediately start puttering around the kitchen making coffee and accidentally dropping spoons or clanking mugs—to the minute I finally crash at night. It's just constant. \n\nAnd as for extremely productive... boy, I wish! If I had a few days where I was actually extremely productive, I might actually finish that birdhouse I was telling you about. Or I might finally get through the backlog of progress reports for my students. You see, I'm overly active, sure, I'll give you that one. But active does not equal productive. Not in my world, anyway. \n\nJust this past weekend, for instance, I had this whole plan. I was going to grade a stack of math worksheets, clean out the gutters, and organize the recycling in the garage. Sounds like a very productive Saturday, right? Well, I took the worksheets out to the patio table, sat down, graded exactly three of them, and then I noticed the gutters were looking really full of those oak leaves I mentioned earlier. So I went to get the ladder. But while I was getting the ladder from the garage, I saw the recycling bins were overflowing, so I started breaking down cardboard boxes. Halfway through breaking down the boxes, the dog started barking at the mail carrier, so I went to check the mail, and then I ended up talking to my neighbor Bob about his new riding lawnmower for twenty minutes because he was having trouble with the spark plugs. \n\nBy the end of the day, I was completely exhausted. I hadn't stopped moving for ten hours straight. But the gutters were still full, half-ripped cardboard was scattered all over the driveway, and those math worksheets were blowing around on the patio table. \n\nSo, no, I definitely don't have periods where I feel unusually high or get a ton of stuff done. I don't have those... those magical phases where everything clicks and I'm a machine. I'm just constantly buzzing around, always in motion, but it's not a special episode or anything. It's just my normal, chaotic everyday life. I just have this constant hum of doing a little bit of everything all at once, but never really finishing anything. So no, nothing like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant problems in your daily life, such as at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, boy... yes. Yes, they really have. It’s actually gotten to a point where I’m pretty worried about how things are going, to be completely honest with you. \n\nAt work, for instance... you know how I mentioned the IEP meetings and the admin classes earlier, and how I just can't seem to sit still? Well, it’s not just a quirky annoyance anymore. The complaints from the other staff and even some parents have gotten serious enough that my principal actually pulled me into her office last week. She closed the door—which is never a good sign in a school building, let me tell you—and she told me that I was being put on a formal corrective plan. A corrective plan! I’m fifty-eight years old, I’ve been a special education teacher for decades, and now I’ve got paperwork in my file because I can’t stop being a disruption. It was a real blow to my pride, but I see their point. I'm just so all over the place.\n\nAnd it’s definitely spilling over at home, too. My wife, she is an absolute saint, but she is just exhausted. We help out a lot with her elderly mother—getting her groceries, sorting her pills, helping around her house—and my wife actually sat me down the other night and said that doing those caregiving routines is just getting so much harder for her. She told me, point blank, that it’s because she feels like she has to manage my energy on top of everything else. \n\nLike, she’ll be trying to focus on getting her mom’s schedule sorted for the week, and I’ll be pacing around the kitchen, talking a mile a minute, starting to fix a leaky faucet but then leaving all the tools on the counter to go check the mail... and it just overwhelms her. She said I'm like a tornado in a space where she desperately needs some calm. \n\nIt’s just... it’s a terrible feeling. I really feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m trying to help! I really am. I want to be a supportive husband, I want to be a good teacher for these kids, but it seems like the harder I try to pitch in, the more chaos I end up causing for the people around me. They just end up having to manage me, instead of me actually being helpful. So, yes, it's definitely causing some very real problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, childhood. Absolutely childhood. It’s funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day when I was looking at some of my second graders. You see them and you think, well, kids are just energetic, right? But with me... it was always more than just typical kid energy, and it's been there for as long as I can possibly remember. 

My mother—rest her soul, she was a wonderful woman, had the patience of a saint, much like my wife—she used to joke about it all the time. She’d go to those parent-teacher conferences, you know, back in the sixties when they just had the little wooden desks with the inkwells, and she’d come home and tell my dad that apparently, I was teaching the class from my seat. That was her running joke. "He's teaching the class from his seat again, Frank!" Because I was just... I was always out of my chair. Constantly. The teacher would be up at the chalkboard, and I'd be wandering over to the pencil sharpener, or leaning across the aisle to talk to my friends. 

And the talking... oh, the talking. I was always talking, always cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving the directions for the assignment. They'd say, "Okay class, take out your—" and I'd be blurting out, "Take out our math books? What page? Are we doing the evens or odds?" Just completely incapable of waiting my turn or letting them finish a thought. 

So, yes, this is deeply, deeply ingrained in who I am. It was part of who I was long, long before adulthood. I know sometimes people can get scattered when they're under stress, and we certainly have a lot on our plate right now with my wife's mother and all those caregiving routines I was telling you about—getting her groceries, sorting the pills, all of that. But I want to be really clear that this... this constant motion, this inability to sit still and just be quiet, it’s not something that just started with this recent family change. Not at all. It’s been my normal since I was a little boy in elementary school, just buzzing around the room and driving my poor teachers up the wall. It's always been this way.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, doctor, it's... it's just getting to be a bit much lately. I'm a special education teacher, and you know, you'd think after all these years—I'm 58 now—you'd think I'd be slowing down a bit, maybe taking it a little easier, but it's actually the exact opposite. What really brings me in today is this... this overwhelming feeling like I am just constantly running on a motor. It’s exactly like that, just a literal engine humming inside my chest that just won't cut out. \n\nI mean, I’ll be at school, right? And I'll finish up an IEP meeting or get done setting up a lesson for my kids—which takes a lot of energy, let me tell you, keeping up with them—but the second I'm done, boom, I immediately jump into the next thing. I don't even sit down to take a breath. I'm rearranging the desks, I'm pacing the room, I'm organizing the supply closet that I just organized on Tuesday. And you'd think I'd go home and just collapse on the couch, right? But no, I get home and it's the exact same story. I walk through the door, drop my bag, and I'm instantly fixing a hinge on the kitchen cabinet, or I'm out doing yard work in the dark, or... I just leap from one task to the next without ever, ever being able to shut myself off. \n\nIt’s like the off switch is completely broken. I just keep going and going and going. My wife says it's exhausting just watching me, and frankly, it's exhausting being me right now, because I just can't seem to stop moving or find any sort of pause button, no matter how much I might want to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually upbeat, overly confident, or like you don't need as much sleep lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, definitely not upbeat. Honestly, if anything, it's the exact opposite—I'm just so tired of it. It's not like I'm walking around feeling like I'm on top of the world or that I can conquer anything. I don't feel overly confident at all, really. I definitely, definitely need it. I mean, I'm 58 years old, my joints feel it, my lower back absolutely feels it from leaning over those lower desks and chasing after my students all day. I crave a good night's rest. It's not that I feel like I only need two hours of sleep and I'm ready to take on the world—no, no, no. I feel the physical exhaustion right down in my bones. I'm not euphoric or feeling like Superman or anything like that. It's much more of a frustrating feeling, you know? It’s like being trapped on a treadmill that someone else is controlling, and they've cranked the speed all the way up, and you're just trying not to fall flat on your face. So, no, definitely not unusually happy or feeling like I don't need to rest. I desperately want to rest, I just... I just can't seem to make it happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel depressed or hopeless. I mean, I'm frustrated, absolutely. Like I was saying, it's exhausting being this wound up all the time. But it's not a sad feeling, you know? It's not like I'm waking up in the morning and thinking, \"Oh, what's the point of it all?\" or feeling like a dark cloud is hanging over me. I actually really care about my job and my students. Just last week, one of my kids—this little guy who's been struggling so much with his reading comprehension—he finally had this breakthrough moment with a story we were working on, and I felt so genuinely happy and proud of him. I still get a lot of joy out of things like that. \n\nIt's just that the joy is sort of... overshadowed, I guess, by this constant need to be doing something else at the same time. Like, I was thrilled for him, but while I was telling him \"good job,\" I was also simultaneously rearranging the dry erase markers on the whiteboard ledge and thinking about how I needed to go fix that squeaky wheel on the AV cart down the hall. So, no, I don't feel down or hopeless in that heavy, depressed kind of way. It's really just the physical and mental toll of not being able to stop moving that's getting to me. I still look forward to things, I still enjoy my wife's cooking, I still like sitting on the porch—well, trying to sit on the porch—it's just the motor won't quit. But no, not depressed. Just tired of the motor.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts or feeling driven to perform certain behaviors over and over again, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not like that. I know what you mean, like... like having to wash your hands a dozen times or checking the stove over and over before you can leave the house. My brother-in-law, actually, he used to do that thing where he'd tap the doorframe three times before he walked through it, every single time. Drove my sister absolutely up the wall, let me tell you. But no, I don't have anything like that. I'm not doing things because I have some... some thought stuck in my head telling me something bad will happen if I don't. \n\nIt's really not about the thoughts at all. It's just the physical need to be doing *something*. Like I said about the supply closet—I'm not organizing it because I'm obsessed with the markers being in color order or because I can't stop thinking about the glue sticks. I organize it because I happen to be standing near it, and my hands just need to be moving, and I can't just stand there doing nothing. If the closet wasn't there, I'd be fixing a chair, or pacing, or, I don't know, wiping down the chalkboard. It doesn't matter what the task is, really, as long as it's a task. \n\nAnd my thoughts... well, they aren't stuck on one thing, that's for sure. If anything, they're bouncing all over the place just like I am. I'll be thinking about a student's reading level, and then suddenly I'm thinking about whether I need to change the oil in the lawnmower, and then I'm wondering if we have enough coffee filters at home. It's just a constant stream of different things, never the same thought over and over like a broken record. So, no, I wouldn't say I have those intrusive thoughts or feel forced to do the same exact behavior repeatedly. I just... I just have to keep moving, whatever that movement happens to be.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was unusually elevated, irritable, or you felt more energetic than usual for several days in a row, to the point where others noticed you were different?\n\nPatient: Elevated or more energetic than usual for a few days? No, no. Because, well, what's \"usual\" for me, right? My \"usual\" is already running at a hundred miles an hour. It's not like I have these... these spells, or phases, where suddenly for three or four days I'm bouncing off the walls and then I go back to being calm. The bouncing off the walls *is* the normal. Honestly, if I suddenly sat still for three days, *that* would be when people would notice I was different! My wife, we've been married thirty-two years, and she always jokes that she's been waiting for my batteries to run out since 1991. So it's not a sudden spike in energy that comes out of nowhere. It's just... always there. Every single day. \n\nAnd as for irritable... I mean, sure, I get irritable, but it's entirely situational. It's not a mood that just takes over my life for days on end. Like, I get incredibly irritable if I'm stuck in one of those mandatory district professional development seminars. You know the ones? Where they cram fifty of us teachers into a stuffy school library, the chairs are those awful hard plastic things, and the presenter is just reading word-for-word off a PowerPoint slide for three hours straight? Oh, I absolutely can't stand that. My knee starts bouncing, I'm clicking my pen, I'm getting up to \"use the restroom\" or get water four or five times just to walk down the hall... I get very irritable then, because I just feel completely trapped in that chair. \n\nBut the minute that meeting is over and I can get back to my classroom and start setting up the sensory bins for my kids or organizing the reading corner, the irritability is completely gone. It vanishes. So it's not a several-day mood thing, it's just me reacting to having to sit still. And I'm definitely not walking around feeling \"elevated\" or like I won the lottery. Like I was telling you before, I'm just exhausted by it all. The energy is physical, it's just this constant motor running in the background. Monday through Sunday, fifty-two weeks a year. So, no, I haven't had any distinct periods where my mood or energy suddenly shifted like you're talking about. It's just the same old exhausting baseline.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting two weeks or more when you felt down or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? No. No, like I was saying a few minutes ago when you asked about feeling depressed, I really don't get like that. I've definitely never had a stretch of two whole weeks where I just felt down or stopped caring about things. \n\nHonestly, it’s the exact opposite when it comes to my interests. I never lose interest in things—if anything, I have *too many* interests and I just can't stick to one for very long before I'm up and doing something else. Take my garage, for example. I love woodworking. Always have. I've been building this birdhouse—well, it started as a birdhouse, then I decided it was going to be a whole elaborate feeding station for the blue jays we get in the backyard. My wife, she loves watching the birds from the kitchen window while she has her morning tea, so I figured, why not make something really nice for her to look at, right? \n\nAnyway, I haven't lost interest in it at all. I still want to finish it. I still love the idea of it. But I'll be out there sanding the roof of this thing, and then I'll notice the weed whacker needs new string, so I start doing that, and then I see a crack in the driveway that needs patching... but the *interest* in the woodworking is still 100 percent there! I never wake up and think, \"I don't care about the birds anymore\" or \"I don't want to go to work.\" I love my students. I told you about that little guy with his reading breakthrough earlier—no, I never lose that spark for the things I care about. \n\nSo a whole two weeks of just feeling down and not caring? No, never. I mean, sure, I might have a bad day here and there, who doesn't? Like when the school board cut our budget for special education sensory tools last year. I was pretty upset for a couple of days, fighting with the administration, writing emails late into the night, pacing around the living room trying to figure out how I was going to afford the supplies out of my own pocket. But I didn't lose interest or get depressed. If anything, I was more fired up. I just don't have that... that shut-down setting, I guess. I'm always on. Even when I'm completely exhausted from being on, I'm still on. So no, no two-week slumps for me. I just keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced things like hearing voices that others don’t hear, or seeing things that others don’t see? Or maybe had thoughts that felt very strange or not your own?\n\nPatient: Voices? Oh, no, no, nothing like that. The only voices I hear are my students, and that’s just because when you spend all day with a room full of kids who need a lot of extra support, their questions just sort of echo in your ears even after you clock out. You know, like when you leave a loud concert and you can still hear the bass? It’s like that, but with questions about fractions. But actual voices that no one else can hear? No, never. \n\nAnd seeing things, definitely not. Honestly, I have a hard enough time keeping track of the things that actually are there! Half the time I’m looking for my keys or my favorite red grading pen, and they’re right in front of my face. If I started seeing things that weren't even real on top of all that, I don't know how I'd ever get out the door in the morning. \n\nAs for the thoughts... you mean like someone else is putting them in my head? No, they are all mine. Unfortunately, they are all mine. I was actually just saying to my wife the other evening—she was sitting out on the patio with her tea, and I was sweeping the deck because the oak tree out back just drops these little twigs everywhere, it’s a never-ending battle keeping that wood clean—and I told her that I wished I could just hand my brain over to someone else for the weekend. Just let someone else drive for a bit so I could get some peace. Because my thoughts are just so crowded. But they are definitely my thoughts. It’s just that there are a hundred of them at once. I’ll be thinking about whether I need to rotate the tires on the sedan, and then I’m worrying about a student’s individualized education plan for Thursday, and then I’m trying to remember if we have enough dog food. It’s all very normal, boring stuff, just happening way too fast. None of it feels alien or strange or anything like that. So no, I’ve never had anything like what you’re talking about. Just the usual traffic jam up there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, even if you're not necessarily feeling sad or down?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Ha! No, no, I don't think I even have a slow gear. If anything, my wife says I'm like a wind-up toy that someone overwound and broke the knob off. But trouble sitting still? Oh, absolutely. That’s... well, that’s just the story of my life right there. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about those professional development days, it’s just impossible for me to just exist in a chair. Especially in IEP meetings—Individualized Education Program meetings, you know, where we sit down with the parents, the school psychologist, maybe the speech therapist... these meetings are incredibly important for the kids. They really map out their whole academic year and what accommodations they need, so I really want to be completely focused and professional. But my body just will not cooperate. \n\nWe’ll be sitting around that big fake-wood conference table in the main office, and my leg is just bouncing under the table. Just going a mile a minute. Or I’m clicking a pen. Click-click-click. And I’m shifting in the chair constantly—crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning forward, leaning back, adjusting my posture. I do it so much that people actually glance over at me. The parents or the principal will just stop talking for a second and look, and it's only when they give me that look that I even realize I’m doing it! I don't even know I'm moving until someone's eyes lock onto my clicking pen or my shaking knee. \n\nAnd it’s not just the IEP meetings, it happens in my admin classes, too. You know, the continuing education classes we have to take to keep our teaching certificates current? Same exact thing. I'm just vibrating in the seat the whole time, trying to find a comfortable position that doesn't exist. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I'm fifty-eight years old, I should be able to sit through a forty-five-minute meeting without squirming like one of my second graders, but I just... I can't. The energy just has to go somewhere. So, definitely not moving slowly, no, but sitting still is pretty much a monumental task for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life when you felt unusually high, overly active, or extremely productive for several days or more, even if it didn’t last very long?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or extremely productive? Gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm always high energy, like I was just saying about the meetings and the bouncing leg and all that. That's just my baseline. It's not some special period that comes and goes for a few days, you know? It's every single day. From the minute I wake up at 5:30 in the morning—and I always wake up at 5:30, usually before the alarm even goes off, much to my wife's dismay because I immediately start puttering around the kitchen making coffee and accidentally dropping spoons or clanking mugs—to the minute I finally crash at night. It's just constant. \n\nAnd as for extremely productive... boy, I wish! If I had a few days where I was actually extremely productive, I might actually finish that birdhouse I was telling you about. Or I might finally get through the backlog of progress reports for my students. You see, I'm overly active, sure, I'll give you that one. But active does not equal productive. Not in my world, anyway. \n\nJust this past weekend, for instance, I had this whole plan. I was going to grade a stack of math worksheets, clean out the gutters, and organize the recycling in the garage. Sounds like a very productive Saturday, right? Well, I took the worksheets out to the patio table, sat down, graded exactly three of them, and then I noticed the gutters were looking really full of those oak leaves I mentioned earlier. So I went to get the ladder. But while I was getting the ladder from the garage, I saw the recycling bins were overflowing, so I started breaking down cardboard boxes. Halfway through breaking down the boxes, the dog started barking at the mail carrier, so I went to check the mail, and then I ended up talking to my neighbor Bob about his new riding lawnmower for twenty minutes because he was having trouble with the spark plugs. \n\nBy the end of the day, I was completely exhausted. I hadn't stopped moving for ten hours straight. But the gutters were still full, half-ripped cardboard was scattered all over the driveway, and those math worksheets were blowing around on the patio table. \n\nSo, no, I definitely don't have periods where I feel unusually high or get a ton of stuff done. I don't have those... those magical phases where everything clicks and I'm a machine. I'm just constantly buzzing around, always in motion, but it's not a special episode or anything. It's just my normal, chaotic everyday life. I just have this constant hum of doing a little bit of everything all at once, but never really finishing anything. So no, nothing like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been causing you significant problems in your daily life, such as at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Oh, boy... yes. Yes, they really have. It’s actually gotten to a point where I’m pretty worried about how things are going, to be completely honest with you. \n\nAt work, for instance... you know how I mentioned the IEP meetings and the admin classes earlier, and how I just can't seem to sit still? Well, it’s not just a quirky annoyance anymore. The complaints from the other staff and even some parents have gotten serious enough that my principal actually pulled me into her office last week. She closed the door—which is never a good sign in a school building, let me tell you—and she told me that I was being put on a formal corrective plan. A corrective plan! I’m fifty-eight years old, I’ve been a special education teacher for decades, and now I’ve got paperwork in my file because I can’t stop being a disruption. It was a real blow to my pride, but I see their point. I'm just so all over the place.\n\nAnd it’s definitely spilling over at home, too. My wife, she is an absolute saint, but she is just exhausted. We help out a lot with her elderly mother—getting her groceries, sorting her pills, helping around her house—and my wife actually sat me down the other night and said that doing those caregiving routines is just getting so much harder for her. She told me, point blank, that it’s because she feels like she has to manage my energy on top of everything else. \n\nLike, she’ll be trying to focus on getting her mom’s schedule sorted for the week, and I’ll be pacing around the kitchen, talking a mile a minute, starting to fix a leaky faucet but then leaving all the tools on the counter to go check the mail... and it just overwhelms her. She said I'm like a tornado in a space where she desperately needs some calm. \n\nIt’s just... it’s a terrible feeling. I really feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m trying to help! I really am. I want to be a supportive husband, I want to be a good teacher for these kids, but it seems like the harder I try to pitch in, the more chaos I end up causing for the people around me. They just end up having to manage me, instead of me actually being helpful. So, yes, it's definitely causing some very real problems.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these kinds of difficulties—like trouble sitting still, being constantly on the go, or having trouble focusing—was this something you experienced even as a child, or did it start more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, childhood. Absolutely childhood. It’s funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day when I was looking at some of my second graders. You see them and you think, well, kids are just energetic, right? But with me... it was always more than just typical kid energy, and it's been there for as long as I can possibly remember. \n\nMy mother—rest her soul, she was a wonderful woman, had the patience of a saint, much like my wife—she used to joke about it all the time. She’d go to those parent-teacher conferences, you know, back in the sixties when they just had the little wooden desks with the inkwells, and she’d come home and tell my dad that apparently, I was teaching the class from my seat. That was her running joke. \"He's teaching the class from his seat again, Frank!\" Because I was just... I was always out of my chair. Constantly. The teacher would be up at the chalkboard, and I'd be wandering over to the pencil sharpener, or leaning across the aisle to talk to my friends. \n\nAnd the talking... oh, the talking. I was always talking, always cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving the directions for the assignment. They'd say, \"Okay class, take out your—\" and I'd be blurting out, \"Take out our math books? What page? Are we doing the evens or odds?\" Just completely incapable of waiting my turn or letting them finish a thought. \n\nSo, yes, this is deeply, deeply ingrained in who I am. It was part of who I was long, long before adulthood. I know sometimes people can get scattered when they're under stress, and we certainly have a lot on our plate right now with my wife's mother and all those caregiving routines I was telling you about—getting her groceries, sorting the pills, all of that. But I want to be really clear that this... this constant motion, this inability to sit still and just be quiet, it’s not something that just started with this recent family change. Not at all. It’s been my normal since I was a little boy in elementary school, just buzzing around the room and driving my poor teachers up the wall. It's always been this way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So, no, I wouldn't say I have those intrusive thoughts or feel forced to do the same exact behavior repeatedly. I just...
