She closed the door—which is never a good sign in a school building, let me tell you—and she told me that I was being put on a formal corrective plan. A corrective plan! I’m fifty-eight years old, I’ve been a special education teacher for decades, and now I’ve got paperwork in my file because I can’t stop being a disruption. It was a real blow to my pride, but I see their point. I'm just so all over the place.\n\nAnd it’s definitely spilling over at home, too. My wife, she is an absolute saint, but she is just exhausted. We help out a lot with her elderly mother—getting her groceries, sorting her pills, helping around her house—and my wife actually sat me down the other night and said that doing those caregiving routines is just getting so much harder for her. She told me, point blank, that it’s because she feels like she has to manage my energy on top of everything else. \n\nLike, she’ll be trying to focus on getting her mom’s schedule sorted for the week, and I’ll be pacing around the kitchen, talking a mile a minute, starting to fix a leaky faucet but then leaving all the tools on the counter to go check the mail... and it just overwhelms her. She said I'm like a tornado in a space where she desperately needs some calm. \n\nIt’s just... it’s a terrible feeling. I really feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m trying to help! I really am. I want to be a supportive husband, I want to be a good teacher for these kids, but it seems like the harder I try to pitch in, the more chaos I end up causing for the people around me. They just end up having to manage me, instead of me actually being helpful. So, yes, it's definitely causing some very real problems.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these kinds of difficulties—like trouble sitting still, being constantly on the go, or having trouble focusing—was this something you experienced even as a child, or did it start more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, childhood. Absolutely childhood. It’s funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day when I was looking at some of my second graders. You see them and you think, well, kids are just energetic, right? But with me... it was always more than just typical kid energy, and it's been there for as long as I can possibly remember. \n\nMy mother—rest her soul, she was a wonderful woman, had the patience of a saint, much like my wife—she used to joke about it all the time. She’d go to those parent-teacher conferences, you know, back in the sixties when they just had the little wooden desks with the inkwells, and she’d come home and tell my dad that apparently, I was teaching the class from my seat. That was her running joke. \"He's teaching the class from his seat again, Frank!\" Because I was just... I was always out of my chair. Constantly. The teacher would be up at the chalkboard, and I'd be wandering over to the pencil sharpener, or leaning across the aisle to talk to my friends. \n\nAnd the talking... oh, the talking. I was always talking, always cutting in before the teacher had even finished giving the directions for the assignment. They'd say, \"Okay class, take out your—\" and I'd be blurting out, \"Take out our math books? What page? Are we doing the evens or odds?\" Just completely incapable of waiting my turn or letting them finish a thought. \n\nSo, yes, this is deeply, deeply ingrained in who I am. It was part of who I was long, long before adulthood. I know sometimes people can get scattered when they're under stress, and we certainly have a lot on our plate right now with my wife's mother and all those caregiving routines I was telling you about—getting her groceries, sorting the pills, all of that. But I want to be really clear that this... this constant motion, this inability to sit still and just be quiet, it’s not something that just started with this recent family change. Not at all. It’s been my normal since I was a little boy in elementary school, just buzzing around the room and driving my poor teachers up the wall. It's always been this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you continued to experience these symptoms—like trouble sitting still, always being on the go, or having trouble focusing— throughout your adult life, or was there ever a period when you didn’t have them?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, the energy itself, the bouncing leg, the talking—that’s always been there. I’ve never had a period in my adult life where I was just a calm, sit-still, read-a-book-by-the-fire kind of guy. My twenties, my thirties, my forties... I was always the guy taking on three extra committees at school and pacing the sidelines at my kids' soccer games. So the constant motion hasn't ever stopped. I've never had a phase where I didn't have that going on.\n\nBut... if we're talking about the actual *trouble* it's causing? The severity of it? The corrective action plan at school, my wife being completely at her wit's end, the feeling that I'm just a tornado ruining everything... that hasn't been my whole adult life. We used to have a rhythm that worked, you know? I’d tire myself out with yard work or school projects, and we managed. But this specific level of trouble, where it feels like everything is just falling apart and I can't keep my head above water... that’s really been going on for about eight months now. \n\nAnd I know exactly why it’s been eight months, because that’s when our older relative—my wife's mother—actually moved in with us. Before that, we were just going over to her place, like I mentioned, getting her groceries and sorting her pills at her own house. But about eight months ago, she had a bit of a scare, and we realized she just couldn't live on her own anymore. So we moved her into our guest room. I spent a whole weekend clearing out all my old teaching supplies, painting the walls this soft yellow color because she loves yellow, getting the room all set up with a special bed and everything. \n\nBut man, ever since she actually moved in under our roof eight months ago, the whole dynamic of the house just shifted completely. Suddenly, my usual running around wasn't just me being my typical self; it was disrupting her quiet time, it was interfering with her daily routines, and my poor wife suddenly had to manage both of us in the same house twenty-four seven. The stress just skyrocketed, and I think that's why it's spilling over into my work now, too, because I'm just so wound up about what's happening at home. \n\nSo, the baseline energy? Always there, never went away. But this severe level of trouble where it's actually breaking down my life and my relationships? That's been exactly eight months, right since she moved in with us.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, or lost interest or pleasure in most activities?\n\nPatient: Down? Like, depressed? No, no, I really wouldn't say that. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get frustrated, especially lately with everything I just told you about—the corrective plan at school, the tension at home since my mother-in-law moved into that yellow guest room. It’s a lot of pressure, and I certainly feel guilty about the chaos I’m causing. But actually feeling sad or down most of the day? Losing interest in things? No, definitely not. \n\nIf anything, my problem is that I have too much interest in things! I never lose my drive to get up and do stuff. Just this past weekend, for example, I was supposed to be just taking it easy, giving my wife some space, but I woke up and decided I was going to finally build this raised garden bed she’d mentioned wanting months ago. I drove all the way to the big home improvement store across town—the one on 4th street, not the local one, because the local one never has the right cedar planks—and I got all the wood, brought it back, started measuring everything out on the driveway. Now, of course, I got sidetracked halfway through because I looked up and noticed the gutters were packed with leaves, so I dragged the ladder out to fix that and completely abandoned the wood... but the point is, the enthusiasm is always there. I still love my projects, I still love being active, I still love teaching my students, even on the hardest days. \n\nI’ve always been a glass-half-full kind of guy, really. Even when things are incredibly chaotic, I don't get that heavy, dark, sit-in-the-dark-and-stare-at-the-wall kind of feeling. I don't think I could sit still long enough to feel that way even if I tried! I get wound up, I get anxious about letting people down, absolutely. But a two-week stretch of just feeling down or not enjoying life? No, I haven't experienced that. I’m always looking for the next thing to do, the next project to start, or the next way to try and fix whatever mess I've made. So, no, I wouldn't say I've ever had a period like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt unusually energized, needed very little sleep, or were more talkative than usual, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized? Well, see, the key word in that question is \"unusually.\" Because if you were to ask my wife, or any of the other teachers in the special ed department, they’d probably tell you I’m unusually energized every single day of my life! But for me, personally? No, I wouldn’t say I’ve ever had a specific period where it suddenly spiked out of nowhere for a few days. It’s always just been this constant, steady hum. \n\nLike, with the sleep thing you mentioned. I sleep alright, you know, I usually get my six or seven hours most nights. Sure, sometimes I toss and turn because my brain won't shut off—like just last night I was lying there for an hour trying to redesign a visual schedule for one of my kids, this sweet boy who just cannot transition from math to reading without a complete meltdown, and I was picturing laminating these little Velcro cards and color-coding them... but I don't have times where I only sleep for an hour or two and feel like I can conquer the world. I still need my sleep. I've never gone days without it or felt like I didn't need it. \n\nAnd as for being more talkative than usual... I mean, look at me! I think we’ve pretty well established today that I’m always talking. I don't think it's physically possible for me to be *more* talkative than my baseline, because my baseline is already talking a mile a minute. My wife always jokes that I probably talk to the dog when she leaves the house, and she's not wrong, I absolutely do. I'll just be standing in the kitchen narrating how I'm making my turkey sandwich to our golden retriever. \n\nSo, no, no sudden bursts or distinct phases of it. It’s just... it’s just the same old me, twenty-four seven, three hundred and sixty-five days a year. I don't get these sudden episodes where I'm suddenly a completely different, super-charged person for a week. I'm just regular-charged, which is apparently a whole lot for everyone else to handle, especially lately, but it never really fluctuates into something completely out of the blue.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when your mood was so low or you felt so down that you needed to stop doing your usual activities, even for a few days?\n\nPatient: No, no, honestly, it goes right back to what I was just saying a minute ago about not really getting depressed. Stopping my usual activities? I don't even know what that would look like for me. I mean, if I stopped doing my usual activities, I think my wife would probably call an ambulance and check my pulse! \n\nBecause even when I'm completely overwhelmed—like, take last Tuesday for instance. It was just one of those days where everything that could go wrong, did. The school's copier jammed right before my first period, which meant I didn't have the modified worksheets for my resource room kids, and then my mother-in-law called my cell phone three times during my only prep period because she couldn't figure out the new universal remote control I bought her for the TV in the guest room. I was so stressed I felt like my head was going to literally pop off my shoulders, and I definitely felt terrible about the whole situation, just feeling like I'm failing at school and failing at home. \n\nBut did I stop? Did I just lay down and quit or stay home the next day? No way. I ended up running to the corner store on my lunch break, buying a bunch of dry-erase markers out of my own pocket, and turning the whole reading lesson into a giant whiteboard game for the kids. And then I went home that evening and spent two hours color-coding that TV remote with different strips of electrical tape so she could use it. \n\nSo, yeah, no matter how bad or guilty or stressed I feel about the chaos I'm causing, I never just shut down or stop doing things. I just keep moving. Sometimes I move in the completely wrong direction and make a bigger mess of things, sure, but I'm always moving. So no, I've never had a time where I was so low that I just stopped functioning or stayed in bed for a few days. That's just not how I'm wired at all."
